People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

The voices and fixed guilty beliefs only show up when I’m in this low, guilty state.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still showing up to clinic, but I’ve had to ask for help with non-urgent administrative work and I avoid social plans because I don’t trust my judgment or energy. It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and a month, starting after I received the clinic award.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. I keep replaying small things I did, like little imperfections, and it turns into this thought that I shouldn’t be trusted… that I might be a dangerous doctor.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have to do certain things over and over to reduce your anxiety about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I wouldn’t say I have a specific thing I do over and over, not really. It’s more that the thoughts just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to behave in certain ways to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I might try to be extra careful, but I don’t think there’s a set behavior I have to do to make the anxiety go away. It’s more just… in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to your thoughts about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I don’t feel driven to repeat something like that. It’s more just the thoughts being there, and I get stuck with them.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and feelings about being a dangerous doctor interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still going to clinic, but I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent admin stuff. And I’ve been avoiding seeing people outside work. I just don’t really trust my judgment or my energy lately, so… it’s starting to affect things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I keep replaying small things I did, like little imperfections, and it turns into this thought that I shouldn’t be trusted… that I might be a dangerous doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over to reduce your anxiety about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I haven’t really noticed a different kind of anxiety, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are a part of yourself, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s me thinking it… not like a voice from outside or anything. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Even when the chart looks fine, I still think some delay or message I sent could’ve quietly harmed a patient, and eventually everyone will see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are in line with your own beliefs and values?\n\nPatient: Not really. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been feeling like I don’t really deserve the trust people put in me. I keep going over small things at work, little clinical imperfections, and it feels like they prove I’m… a dangerous doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly about work. I keep replaying small things I did, like little imperfections, and it turns into this thought that I shouldn’t be trusted… that I might be a dangerous doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over to reduce your anxiety about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a specific thing I do over and over, not really. It’s more that the thoughts just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to behave in certain ways to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I might try to be extra careful, but I don’t think there’s a set behavior I have to do to make the anxiety go away. It’s more just… in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to your thoughts about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I don’t feel driven to repeat something like that. It’s more just the thoughts being there, and I get stuck with them.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and feelings about being a dangerous doctor interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still going to clinic, but I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent admin stuff. And I’ve been avoiding seeing people outside work. I just don’t really trust my judgment or my energy lately, so… it’s starting to affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something specific you need to think about or do when these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor come up, to make them go away or feel better?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing. I might sort of… go over it in my head, but it doesn’t really make it go away. I’m not doing some particular routine or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts to prevent something bad from happening when you have these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. It’s not like I have to think something specific to stop something happening. The thoughts just… sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when interacting with others, outside of the thoughts about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can be a bit tense around people lately, but I wouldn’t say it’s separate from all that. I haven’t really noticed a different kind of anxiety, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are a part of yourself, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s me thinking it… not like a voice from outside or anything. I might look over something to be sure, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck checking it over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It’s mostly the same kind of thing looping — that I don’t really deserve people trusting me, and I keep going back over small clinical things like they prove I’m dangerous somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are reasonable or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I know it probably sounds irrational when I say it out loud. It has started to affect how reliably I function at work and how connected I am outside of it.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about a year and a month, starting after I received the clinic award.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 30 / physician / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S030\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about these thoughts to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it’s to prevent something, exactly. It feels more like… if I don’t keep going over it, I’m missing the thing that proves I hurt someone. Even when the chart looks fine, I still think some delay or message I sent could’ve quietly harmed a patient, and eventually everyone will see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are in line with your own beliefs and values?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean… I don’t want to be that kind of doctor. I care about doing right by people.\n\nBut I keep feeling like I don’t deserve the trust they put in me, and every small mistake or imperfect thing I did feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are unwanted and pop into your mind without you trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess they are unwanted. I’m not trying to think about it.\n\nIt’s more like I’ll be at work and then I start replaying some small clinical thing I didn’t do perfectly, and it turns into… maybe that proves I shouldn’t be trusted, or that I’m dangerous somehow. I don’t want to think that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you have to mentally or physically do something to try to make these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor go away or to prevent the bad things you worry about from happening?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I keep going over small things at work, little clinical imperfections, and it feels like they prove I’m… a dangerous doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having repetitive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Yes… mostly about work. I keep replaying small things I did, like little imperfections, and it turns into this thought that I shouldn’t be trusted… that I might be a dangerous doctor.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to do certain things over and over to reduce your anxiety about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: I… don’t know. I wouldn’t say I have a specific thing I do over and over, not really. It’s more that the thoughts just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to behave in certain ways to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I might try to be extra careful, but I don’t think there’s a set behavior I have to do to make the anxiety go away. It’s more just… in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to repeat certain actions or mental acts in response to your thoughts about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I don’t feel driven to repeat something like that. It’s more just the thoughts being there, and I get stuck with them.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and feelings about being a dangerous doctor interfered with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I’m still going to clinic, but I’ve had to ask for help with some non-urgent admin stuff. And I’ve been avoiding seeing people outside work. I just don’t really trust my judgment or my energy lately, so… it’s starting to affect things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there's something specific you need to think about or do when these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor come up, to make them go away or feel better?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s a specific thing. I might sort of… go over it in my head, but it doesn’t really make it go away. I’m not doing some particular routine or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you must think certain thoughts to prevent something bad from happening when you have these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. It’s not like I have to think something specific to stop something happening. The thoughts just… sit there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when interacting with others, outside of the thoughts about being a dangerous doctor?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I can be a bit tense around people lately, but I wouldn’t say it’s separate from all that. I haven’t really noticed a different kind of anxiety, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are a part of yourself, or do they feel like they're coming from outside of yourself?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s me thinking it… not like a voice from outside or anything. But I can’t really shake it. Even if I check the chart and there’s nothing there, I still believe I must have harmed someone somehow… with a delay or a message that wasn’t right. Eventually people will find it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to check things repeatedly to make sure everything is okay?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not repeatedly, exactly. I might look over something to be sure, but I wouldn’t say I’m stuck checking it over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess so. It’s mostly the same kind of thing looping — that I don’t really deserve people trusting me, and I keep going back over small clinical things like they prove I’m dangerous somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are reasonable or do they seem irrational to you?\n\nPatient: I know it probably sounds irrational when I say it out loud. But it doesn’t feel that way to me. Even when I check the chart and there’s nothing showing anything bad happened, I still can’t shake the belief that some small delay or imperfect message from me hurt someone, and that eventually people will find out.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to think about these imperfections at work for a certain amount of time to feel better?\n\nPatient: Not really a set amount of time, no. Even when the chart looks fine, I still think some delay or message I sent could’ve quietly harmed a patient, and eventually everyone will see it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are in line with your own beliefs and values?\n\nPatient: Not really. I care about doing right by people.\n\nBut I keep feeling like I don’t deserve the trust they put in me, and every small mistake or imperfect thing I did feels like proof that I’m dangerous somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts about being a dangerous doctor are unwanted and pop into your mind without you trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess they are unwanted.
